What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, what brings me in is... well, it's my eating. You see, once I start eating those comfort foods, I tell myself, 'Maria, that is enough,' but my hand still goes back to the pot like I can’t make myself stop. It's just like how my mother used to be, God rest her soul. She would always say she was full, but then she'd be back in the kitchen an hour later making a whole new pot of rice. She was a wonderful cook, though. Oh, the tamales she used to make for Christmas... they took all day, but they were worth it. We'd have the whole family over, all my cousins and aunts, and the house would smell like corn and chili for days. Anyway, I just... I can't seem to stop myself once I start.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after eating those comfort foods?\n\nPatient: Oh, yes. Afterward I feel so ashamed and sad. It's like... like I turned something that used to mean family and caring into something I have to hide from people at church. I'd just die if they found out. Father Michael at our parish, he's always talking about honesty and living in the light, and he's such a good man. He came to visit right away when my husband passed away. Brought a lovely green bean casserole, though between you and me, it needed a pinch more salt. He’s been looking a little tired lately, honestly, the poor man. I think the diocese gives him too many parishes to look after, driving back and forth all Sunday morning. I told my friend Rosa we should knit him a nice warm sweater for the winter. Rosa's arthritis has been so bad she can barely hold the needles, though... but yes, I just feel terrible and guilty when I'm sitting there alone looking at the empty wrappers.\n\nDoctor: Does this happen to you often, or was it just a one-time thing?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, doctor, it’s not just a one-time thing. I wish it was. Ever since my dear friend passed away... it’s been happening most weeks. Usually once or twice a week, mostly on those quiet afternoons after I get back from choir practice or finishing up my gardening. \n\nChoir just hasn't been the same without her, to be honest. We used to sit together in the alto section for twenty years. Now they have me sitting next to Mrs. Higgins, and bless her heart, but she sings terribly flat. And the director insists on all these modern songs lately. I don't know why we can't just stick to the traditional hymns, they're so much more beautiful and respectful. \n\nAnd my garden... well, I was out there pulling weeds yesterday and noticed the squirrels have been getting into my tomatoes again. They take one little bite out of the ripest, most beautiful red ones and just leave them sitting there on the dirt! It makes me so mad. I tried putting up a little wire fence my neighbor gave me, but those little menaces just climb right over it like it's nothing. \n\nBut... what was I saying? Oh, right. Yes, it's usually those quiet afternoons when I come inside and I'm all alone in the house that I end up eating like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since this eating behavior started?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, I haven't really been paying much attention to the scale lately. I suppose my clothes fit about the same, maybe a little snug, but it's hard to tell. I mostly wear those elastic-waist slacks nowadays anyway. I buy them from that catalog that comes in the mail... oh, what is it called? Blair! Yes, Blair. They have the loveliest pastel colors for spring. I ordered a beautiful seafoam green pair last month, but when they arrived, the package was all torn up. I had to call the post office to complain, and the young man on the phone—Kevin, I think his name was—he was very polite, but he didn't seem to know what he was doing. It's like nobody takes pride in their work anymore. The mail carrier we used to have, Mr. Henderson, he was wonderful. He'd always make sure the packages were tucked safely behind the screen door so they wouldn't get rained on. He retired two years ago and moved to Florida to be near his grandchildren. I sent him a Christmas card last year, but I'm not sure if he got it. But, no, I haven't really noticed any big changes with my weight.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since this eating behavior started?\n\nPatient: Lost weight? Oh, heavens no. Definitely haven't lost any. Like I said, if anything, my clothes are a little tighter. But no, I haven't lost a single pound. \n\nNow, my sister-in-law Martha, she lost a whole lot of weight last year. She was doing one of those liquid diets, drinking those shakes... oh, what are they called? SlimFast! Yes. She drank the strawberry ones every single day. I told her, Martha, you need real food, you can't just live on strawberry milk. But she's always been so stubborn. Even when we were younger, back in the fifties, she would insist on wearing these dreadful pointy shoes that pinched her toes just because they were in fashion. She completely ruined her feet, you know. Has to wear special orthotics now in all her shoes. They cost her an absolute fortune because Medicare wouldn't cover them. \n\nIt's a crying shame what they won't cover these days, especially for eyes and teeth. I need a new crown on my back molar and Dr. Evans—he's my dentist, wonderful man, always asks about my hydrangeas—he quoted me a price that just about made my heart stop. I don't know how regular folks are supposed to afford it. \n\nBut... oh, what were you asking? Right, my weight. No, doctor, no weight loss for me.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that when you're eating those comfort foods, you're eating a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose it is a lot, now that you mention it. Some of those afternoons after choir practice, or when I come in from pulling those dreadful weeds in the garden, I'll go into the kitchen and fix myself a nice plate of rice and beans, or maybe some leftover chicken stew. But then... I just keep going back for more. I'll have another bowl, and then another, until I've probably eaten enough for two or three full meals in just a little bit of time. \n\nIt reminds me of how my late husband, Arthur, used to eat on Thanksgiving. Oh, Arthur could put away a turkey dinner like nobody's business! He always had to have three slices of my homemade pecan pie. I make it with real butter and dark corn syrup, none of that imitation stuff you get at the grocery store nowadays. The last time I was at the Piggly Wiggly, I couldn't even find real vanilla extract. It was all that artificial flavoring. And the prices! A tiny little bottle cost almost six dollars. I told the cashier—a sweet girl named Brittany, she's studying to be a nurse at the community college—I told her it was highway robbery. She just smiled and bagged my groceries. She always double-bags the heavy things for me, which I appreciate. My paper bags tore once in the driveway, and a jar of sweet pickles shattered everywhere. It took me an hour to hose down the concrete so the neighborhood stray cats wouldn't get sticky paws. \n\nBut yes, doctor, I do eat quite a large amount all at once when I get like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of your eating when you're having those large amounts of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: Well, that's exactly what brings me in, doctor. It's... it's like I have no control at all. Once I start eating those comfort foods, I'll sit there and tell myself, 'Maria, that is enough, you don't need another bite.' But my hand just goes right back to the pot anyway. It's like I can't make myself stop, no matter what I say in my head. \n\nIt's the strangest feeling, almost like when my car started acting up last winter. The brakes just wouldn't catch right. I was driving down Elm Street, right past the old Methodist church where they have those lovely stained glass windows. They restored them back in '98, I think it was. The Ladies Auxiliary raised the money through bake sales. Oh, the bake sales we used to have! My friend Betty would make these lemon bars that were just to die for. She wouldn't give anyone the recipe, took it right to the grave with her. Such a shame. Her daughter tried to recreate them for the church picnic last summer, but they were entirely too tart. I didn't say anything, of course, because you don't want to hurt anyone's feelings, especially at a church picnic. We had it out at Miller's Pond, and the mosquitoes were just terrible. I had bites all up and down my ankles for a week. I tried putting calamine lotion on them, but it just got all over my good floral bedsheets. \n\nBut yes, to answer your question, I don't feel in control at all. I just can't seem to stop myself once I get going.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or ashamed immediately after these episodes of eating large amounts of food?\n\nPatient: Oh, it's just awful. Yes, I do. Afterward, I just sit there and feel so ashamed and... well, just sad, really. Cooking and eating, it always used to mean family, you know? Taking care of Arthur and the boys, showing them I loved them. But now I've turned it into this terrible secret that I feel like I have to hide from everyone, especially the people at church. I'd just die if they knew. \n\nYou know how church ladies can talk. Margaret Higgins, bless her heart, she means well, but she has a mouth like a running faucet. Just last Sunday after service, she was going on and on about the choir director's new haircut. Said it made him look like a plucked chicken! I nearly choked on my peppermint. I always keep a few peppermints in my purse to keep my throat clear during the hymns. The red and white striped ones, you know the kind. Though I bought a bag of the green spearmint ones by mistake last month at the Dollar General. I ended up giving them to my neighbor's grandson, little Timmy. He's such a sweet boy, always helps me bring the empty trash cans back up the driveway on Tuesdays. The sanitation workers always leave them right in the middle of the sidewalk where a person could trip and break a hip. My friend Eleanor broke her hip back in 2015 just walking out to check her mail, and she was never the same. Had to move into one of those assisted living places over in Springfield, the one with the green awnings. \n\nBut, oh dear, what was I saying? Yes, the guilt. It's a very sad, heavy feeling to have to hide what I'm doing like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge or anxiety if you try to stop eating or resist the comfort foods when you're in the middle of one of these episodes?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I wouldn't say it's an overwhelming urge or anxiety, exactly. It's not like that fluttery feeling I get in my chest when I have to speak in front of the congregation. I had to read the announcements last month because Pastor Dave had a terrible head cold. Sounded like he was talking underwater! His wife, Susan, made him this awful-smelling onion syrup. She swears by it, says it clears the sinuses right up. She learned it from her grandmother who came over from Poland in the twenties. \n\nThey lived in Chicago for a long time before moving down here. Chicago! Can you imagine the wind? My sister-in-law went to Chicago once for a convention—she sold Mary Kay cosmetics for years. Had the pink Cadillac and everything. She used to give me all the samples of the lipstick colors that didn't sell well. There was this one shade, 'Frosted Watermelon,' that made my lips look like I'd been eating chalk. I wore it to a PTA meeting once back when my boys were in middle school, and the home economics teacher kept staring at my mouth the whole time. \n\nBut no, doctor, I don't really get anxious or have a strong urge like that if I try to stop. I just... I just don't stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like eating those comfort foods is something you have to do, even if you don't really want to?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I wouldn't say it's like a chore or something I'm being forced to do against my will. Usually, when I start, I really do want it. Like my macaroni and cheese, for instance. I always use the sharp cheddar from the deli counter at Kroger. Not the pre-packaged stuff in the plastic bags, mind you. That kind just never melts right. \n\nI learned that the hard way when I was making a casserole for the neighborhood block party a few years ago. The cashier at the store—this young girl with the brightest blue hair, just the exact color of a blue jay, can you imagine?—she accidentally gave me the mild cheddar instead of the sharp. I didn't want to hold up the line because the man behind me was buying three massive bags of dog food and looked like he was in a terrible rush, so I didn't check the label until I was already grating it at home. Now, my Arthur, God rest his soul, he wouldn't have known the difference. He'd eat just about anything as long as it had enough salt on it. His doctor was always getting after him about the salt shaker. He used to hide it behind the napkin holder whenever Dr. Evans would come up in conversation! Oh, he was a stubborn man, but I miss him terribly. \n\nBut no, to answer your question, I don't feel like I have to eat the food if I don't want it. I usually want it very much. It's just that once I take that first bite, the stopping part is what gets me.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating large amounts of food happen in a typical month?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think... Since my dear friend Eleanor passed away—oh, it's been so hard without her. We used to do everything together. Anyway, since she passed, this has been happening most weeks. Usually once or twice a week. It almost always happens on those quiet afternoons, you know? After I get home from choir practice on Wednesdays, or maybe after I've been out tending to the garden. \n\nMy hydrangeas are just beautiful this year, by the way. The big blue ones. I read in Better Homes and Gardens—or was it Southern Living? I get them both mixed up because the mail carrier, Mr. Henderson, always rubber-bands them together. He's retiring next month after thirty years on the route. His daughter is throwing him a big barbecue over at Centennial Park. She asked me to make my potato salad, the one with the little bits of chopped celery and paprika sprinkled on top. I always use Duke's mayonnaise, never Miracle Whip. Miracle Whip is just too sweet for a proper potato salad, don't you think? My aunt Betty used to put raisins in her potato salad, and I tell you, it was a crime against nature. We had to pretend to like it at every family reunion just to keep the peace. \n\nBut yes, to answer you, it's usually once or twice a week, on those quiet afternoons when the house just feels too empty.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself eating those comfort foods at the same time every day or week, like it's part of a routine?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. It's not like a strict schedule or anything. I don't set a clock for it like I do for my blood pressure pill. Dr. Evans is always so particular about me taking that at exactly 8:00 AM. He says consistency is key. Just like watering the tomatoes. If you don't water them at the same time every morning, before the sun gets too hot, they split right open. \n\nI had the most beautiful crop of Big Boy tomatoes last summer, just bursting on the vine. I canned twenty jars of marinara sauce. It's a recipe I got from my neighbor, Mrs. Rossi, years ago. She used to grow her own basil right on her kitchen windowsill in these little painted teacups. Her husband was a bricklayer, built that whole retaining wall down on Elm Street all by himself. He had hands like catchers' mitts, but he was so gentle when he'd prune her rosebushes. They moved to Florida back in '98, to one of those communities with the golf carts. I can't imagine driving a golf cart to the grocery store, can you? Where would you put all the bags? \n\nBut no, it's not a routine. It just sort of happens when the house is quiet and I'm feeling a bit lonely, not at a specific time.\n\nDoctor: Did you learn this eating behavior from someone in your family or is it something you developed on your own?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't really know if I learned it from anyone. I don't think so. My mother was always very proper about meals. We sat down at the table, all six of us, at exactly six o'clock every evening. She would ring this little brass bell when dinner was ready. It had a wooden handle that was worn smooth from years of use. I actually still have that bell, sitting right on my mantelpiece next to the porcelain dog Arthur won for me at the county fair in 1964. \n\nThat was the year they had the biggest Ferris wheel I'd ever seen. We rode it right at sunset, and you could see all the way out to the Miller farm. The Millers used to grow the sweetest corn. Silver Queen, it was called. You can't find corn like that anymore at the supermarket. It's all that engineered stuff now that tastes like cardboard. Anyway, my mother never would have eaten like I do now. She'd just have a small portion of whatever she cooked, and that was that. So, no, I suppose it's just something that started on my own, especially after things got so quiet around here.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating those comfort foods is a way for you to cope with your loneliness or other emotions?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't rightly know. I haven't really thought about it in those words, like coping with things. I suppose maybe? It's just... well, it's like when I sit down to watch my programs in the evening. I love watching those old black-and-white movies on that classic movie channel. Robert Osborne used to host them, you know. He had such a wonderful, soothing voice. I was just heartbroken when he passed away a few years back. He always knew the most fascinating little pieces of trivia about all the actors. Like Cary Grant! Did you know his real name was Archibald Leach? Just imagine trying to be a big romantic leading man with a name like Archibald. \n\nMy cousin Shirley's husband was named Archibald, but everyone just called him Archie. He ran the little hardware store over on Main Street for years and years, right up until that giant Home Depot moved in out on the highway and ran all the local places out of business. It's a real shame, it is. Archie used to keep a big glass jar of lollipops on the counter for the kids—the kind with the little safety loop handle so they wouldn't choke. They were always cherry or grape. I never cared much for the grape ones myself, they always tasted exactly like that thick purple cough syrup my mother used to make us take when we got the croup. \n\nBut oh, my goodness, I'm wandering again. What were you asking me? Something about my emotions? I'm just not sure, doctor. I just know the house gets awful quiet, and the next thing I know, I'm in the kitchen eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating behavior is something you've developed as a habit over time or is it related to how you were raised or taught to eat?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I kind of already mentioned my mother and how strict she was about our meals. So I don't think it's how I was raised. She would never have stood for anyone eating outside of supper time. She was very particular about manners, too. Elbows off the table, napkin in your lap. We had these lovely cloth napkins, not the paper ones you get nowadays. They were a crisp white linen with a little embroidered bluebird in the corner. My Aunt Clara made them for my mother as a wedding gift. \n\nAunt Clara was a seamstress down at the old textile mill before it closed in the seventies. She could sew anything, just by looking at a picture in a magazine. She made my prom dress, actually. It was a beautiful seafoam green chiffon with a sweetheart neckline. I went to the prom with Tommy Henderson. He drove his father's Buick, and we went to the diner afterward for milkshakes. Tommy ended up joining the Navy right after high school and I think he moved out to California. San Diego, maybe? Or was it San Francisco? I remember he sent me a postcard once with a picture of the Golden Gate Bridge. It always looked more orange than gold to me in the pictures. \n\nBut, oh, I'm sorry, I've lost the thread again. You were asking if it's a habit? I really couldn't say for sure. I just know it's something I do now when the house feels so empty.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you eat those comfort foods as a way to remember happier times or to fill the emptiness you feel?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm not entirely sure about that. Happier times? I suppose food does remind me of the old days sometimes. Like my grandmother's peach cobbler. Oh, she used to make it every Sunday after church during the summer. We went to the First Methodist down on Elm Street. The church had these gorgeous stained glass windows that the morning light would just pour through, making colorful little puddles on the wooden pews. They depicted the feeding of the five thousand, I think. Or maybe it was the wedding at Cana? \n\nReverend Higgins was the pastor back then. He had this booming voice that would wake you right up if you started dozing during the sermon, but he was really the gentlest man. He always kept peppermint candies in his coat pocket for the children. Not the soft ones, mind you, but the hard ones with the little red and white stripes wrapped in clear cellophane. You had to be careful not to bite down on them too soon or you'd crack a tooth! My brother Jimmy did exactly that once, chipped his front tooth right in half the week before school picture day. Mother was absolutely furious with him. She tried to make him smile with his lips closed for the photographer, but he just looked like he was holding his breath or trying not to sneeze. Oh, Jimmy... he was always getting into some sort of scrape. \n\nBut goodness me, I'm rambling on again about Jimmy's teeth. What were we talking about? Filling the emptiness? I really couldn't tell you if that's why I do it, doctor. I haven't figured all that out. I just know I end up eating when there's nothing else going on and the house is just too quiet.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating those comfort foods is something you've learned to do over time as a way to deal with your emotions?\n\nPatient: Learned to do it over time? I really couldn't say. I never was much of a student when it came to learning things, to be perfectly honest. I actually left high school in the tenth grade to help out at home, and then I got a job at the Woolworth's lunch counter down in the square. Oh, they had us wear these stiff pink cotton uniforms with a white apron that you had to iron and starch every single night. If the manager, Mr. Abernathy, saw even a hint of a wrinkle, he'd send you right to the back room. \n\nMr. Abernathy was a very particular man. He wore a silver pocket watch on a chain that he'd pull out and check constantly. He said it belonged to his grandfather, who fought in the Spanish-American War. Or maybe it was World War I? I remember he told us the whole story once when the soda fountain broke down and we had to wait all afternoon for the repairman. The repairman's name was Earl, and he always smelled like peppermint oil and motor grease. He drove this big yellow truck that rattled so loud you could hear him coming from three streets over. \n\nAnyway, I'm sorry, my mind is just a million miles away today. Did you ask if I learned to do it? I just don't know about all that, doctor. I just know that when I'm sitting there by myself in the evening and the television isn't enough to keep me company, I end up getting into the food.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. We had to pretend to like it at every family reunion just to keep the peace. \n\nBut yes, to answer you, it's usually once or twice a week, on those quiet afternoons when the house just feels too empty.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself eating those comfort foods at the same time every day or week, like it's part of a routine?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. It's not like a strict schedule or anything. I don't set a clock for it like I do for my blood pressure pill. Dr. Evans is always so particular about me taking that at exactly 8:00 AM. He says consistency is key. Just like watering the tomatoes. If you don't water them at the same time every morning, before the sun gets too hot, they split right open. \n\nI had the most beautiful crop of Big Boy tomatoes last summer, just bursting on the vine. I canned twenty jars of marinara sauce. It's a recipe I got from my neighbor, Mrs. Rossi, years ago. She used to grow her own basil right on her kitchen windowsill in these little painted teacups. Her husband was a bricklayer, built that whole retaining wall down on Elm Street all by himself. He had hands like catchers' mitts, but he was so gentle when he'd prune her rosebushes. They moved to Florida back in '98, to one of those communities with the golf carts. I can't imagine driving a golf cart to the grocery store, can you? Where would you put all the bags? \n\nBut no, it's not a routine. It just sort of happens when the house is quiet and I'm feeling a bit lonely, not at a specific time.\n\nDoctor: Did you learn this eating behavior from someone in your family or is it something you developed on your own?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't really know if I learned it from anyone. I don't think so. My mother was always very proper about meals. We sat down at the table, all six of us, at exactly six o'clock every evening. She would ring this little brass bell when dinner was ready. It had a wooden handle that was worn smooth from years of use. I actually still have that bell, sitting right on my mantelpiece next to the porcelain dog Arthur won for me at the county fair in 1964. \n\nThat was the year they had the biggest Ferris wheel I'd ever seen. We rode it right at sunset, and you could see all the way out to the Miller farm. The Millers used to grow the sweetest corn. Silver Queen, it was called. You can't find corn like that anymore at the supermarket. It's all that engineered stuff now that tastes like cardboard. Anyway, my mother never would have eaten like I do now. She'd just have a small portion of whatever she cooked, and that was that. So, no, I suppose it's just something that started on my own, especially after things got so quiet around here.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating those comfort foods is a way for you to cope with your loneliness or other emotions?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't rightly know. I haven't really thought about it in those words, like coping with things. I suppose maybe? It's just... well, it's like when I sit down to watch my programs in the evening. I love watching those old black-and-white movies on that classic movie channel. Robert Osborne used to host them, you know. He had such a wonderful, soothing voice. I was just heartbroken when he passed away a few years back. He always knew the most fascinating little pieces of trivia about all the actors. Like Cary Grant! Did you know his real name was Archibald Leach? Just imagine trying to be a big romantic leading man with a name like Archibald. \n\nMy cousin Shirley's husband was named Archibald, but everyone just called him Archie. He ran the little hardware store over on Main Street for years and years, right up until that giant Home Depot moved in out on the highway and ran all the local places out of business. It's a real shame, it is. Archie used to keep a big glass jar of lollipops on the counter for the kids—the kind with the little safety loop handle so they wouldn't choke. They were always cherry or grape. I never cared much for the grape ones myself, they always tasted exactly like that thick purple cough syrup my mother used to make us take when we got the croup. \n\nBut oh, my goodness, I'm wandering again. What were you asking me? Something about my emotions? I'm just not sure, doctor. I just know the house gets awful quiet, and the next thing I know, I'm in the kitchen eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating behavior is something you've developed as a habit over time or is it related to how you were raised or taught to eat?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I kind of already mentioned my mother and how strict she was about our meals. So I don't think it's how I was raised. She would never have stood for anyone eating outside of supper time. She was very particular about manners, too. Elbows off the table, napkin in your lap. We had these lovely cloth napkins, not the paper ones you get nowadays. They were a crisp white linen with a little embroidered bluebird in the corner. My Aunt Clara made them for my mother as a wedding gift. \n\nAunt Clara was a seamstress down at the old textile mill before it closed in the seventies. She could sew anything, just by looking at a picture in a magazine. She made my prom dress, actually. It was a beautiful seafoam green chiffon with a sweetheart neckline. I went to the prom with Tommy Henderson. He drove his father's Buick, and we went to the diner afterward for milkshakes. Tommy ended up joining the Navy right after high school and I think he moved out to California. San Diego, maybe? Or was it San Francisco? I remember he sent me a postcard once with a picture of the Golden Gate Bridge. It always looked more orange than gold to me in the pictures. \n\nBut, oh, I'm sorry, I've lost the thread again. You were asking if it's a habit? I really couldn't say for sure. I just know it's something I do now when the house feels so empty.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you eat those comfort foods as a way to remember happier times or to fill the emptiness you feel?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm not entirely sure about that. Happier times? I suppose food does remind me of the old days sometimes. Like my grandmother's peach cobbler. Oh, she used to make it every Sunday after church during the summer. We went to the First Methodist down on Elm Street. The church had these gorgeous stained glass windows that the morning light would just pour through, making colorful little puddles on the wooden pews. They depicted the feeding of the five thousand, I think. Or maybe it was the wedding at Cana? \n\nReverend Higgins was the pastor back then. He had this booming voice that would wake you right up if you started dozing during the sermon, but he was really the gentlest man. He always kept peppermint candies in his coat pocket for the children. Not the soft ones, mind you, but the hard ones with the little red and white stripes wrapped in clear cellophane. You had to be careful not to bite down on them too soon or you'd crack a tooth! My brother Jimmy did exactly that once, chipped his front tooth right in half the week before school picture day. Mother was absolutely furious with him. She tried to make him smile with his lips closed for the photographer, but he just looked like he was holding his breath or trying not to sneeze. Oh, Jimmy... he was always getting into some sort of scrape. \n\nBut goodness me, I'm rambling on again about Jimmy's teeth. What were we talking about? Filling the emptiness? I really couldn't tell you if that's why I do it, doctor. I haven't figured all that out. I just know I end up eating when there's nothing else going on and the house is just too quiet.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating those comfort foods is something you've learned to do over time as a way to deal with your emotions?\n\nPatient: Learned to do it over time? I really couldn't say. I never was much of a student when it came to learning things, to be perfectly honest. I actually left high school in the tenth grade to help out at home, and then I got a job at the Woolworth's lunch counter down in the square. Oh, they had us wear these stiff pink cotton uniforms with a white apron that you had to iron and starch every single night. If the manager, Mr. Abernathy, saw even a hint of a wrinkle, he'd send you right to the back room. \n\nMr. Abernathy was a very particular man. He wore a silver pocket watch on a chain that he'd pull out and check constantly. He said it belonged to his grandfather, who fought in the Spanish-American War. Or maybe it was World War I? I remember he told us the whole story once when the soda fountain broke down and we had to wait all afternoon for the repairman. The repairman's name was Earl, and he always smelled like peppermint oil and motor grease. He drove this big yellow truck that rattled so loud you could hear him coming from three streets over. \n\nAnyway, I'm sorry, my mind is just a million miles away today. Did you ask if I learned to do it? I just don't know about all that, doctor. I just know that when I'm sitting there by myself in the evening and the television isn't enough to keep me company, I end up getting into the food.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, you're asking me that again, and... I honestly don't know if it's something I learned. Learned to deal with feelings? I don't think people talked about dealing with feelings back when I was coming up. You just got on with things. Like when my husband, Harold, passed. He worked at the post office for thirty-five years. Started out sorting mail in the back room and worked his way up to the front counter. He knew everyone's name in town, and their dogs' names, too. 

He used to carry those little milk-bone biscuits in his pockets for the dogs on his route back when he was walking. The pockets of his uniform trousers would get all crumbly and dusty from them. I spent half my life turning those pockets inside out before putting them in the wash. We had this old wringer washer on the back porch... oh, it was a beast of a machine. You had to be so careful feeding the clothes through the rollers or it would grab your fingers right along with the shirts. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable, caught her whole hand in hers once. She had to wear a cast for six weeks and her daughter had to come over every day to help her pin the laundry to the line. 

But... oh dear, I'm doing it again, aren't I? Going on and on. You were asking about learning to deal with feelings? I just... I don't have a good answer for you. I just know that when I'm feeling lonely, the food is there, and I eat it. I don't know if I learned it or if it just happened.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, what brings me in is... well, it's my eating. You see, once I start eating those comfort foods, I tell myself, 'Maria, that is enough,' but my hand still goes back to the pot like I can’t make myself stop. It's just like how my mother used to be, God rest her soul. She would always say she was full, but then she'd be back in the kitchen an hour later making a whole new pot of rice. She was a wonderful cook, though. Oh, the tamales she used to make for Christmas... they took all day, but they were worth it. We'd have the whole family over, all my cousins and aunts, and the house would smell like corn and chili for days. Anyway, I just... I can't seem to stop myself once I start.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after eating those comfort foods?\n\nPatient: Oh, yes. Afterward I feel so ashamed and sad. It's like... like I turned something that used to mean family and caring into something I have to hide from people at church. I'd just die if they found out. Father Michael at our parish, he's always talking about honesty and living in the light, and he's such a good man. He came to visit right away when my husband passed away. Brought a lovely green bean casserole, though between you and me, it needed a pinch more salt. He’s been looking a little tired lately, honestly, the poor man. I think the diocese gives him too many parishes to look after, driving back and forth all Sunday morning. I told my friend Rosa we should knit him a nice warm sweater for the winter. Rosa's arthritis has been so bad she can barely hold the needles, though... but yes, I just feel terrible and guilty when I'm sitting there alone looking at the empty wrappers.\n\nDoctor: Does this happen to you often, or was it just a one-time thing?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, doctor, it’s not just a one-time thing. I wish it was. Ever since my dear friend passed away... it’s been happening most weeks. Usually once or twice a week, mostly on those quiet afternoons after I get back from choir practice or finishing up my gardening. \n\nChoir just hasn't been the same without her, to be honest. We used to sit together in the alto section for twenty years. Now they have me sitting next to Mrs. Higgins, and bless her heart, but she sings terribly flat. And the director insists on all these modern songs lately. I don't know why we can't just stick to the traditional hymns, they're so much more beautiful and respectful. \n\nAnd my garden... well, I was out there pulling weeds yesterday and noticed the squirrels have been getting into my tomatoes again. They take one little bite out of the ripest, most beautiful red ones and just leave them sitting there on the dirt! It makes me so mad. I tried putting up a little wire fence my neighbor gave me, but those little menaces just climb right over it like it's nothing. \n\nBut... what was I saying? Oh, right. Yes, it's usually those quiet afternoons when I come inside and I'm all alone in the house that I end up eating like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since this eating behavior started?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, I haven't really been paying much attention to the scale lately. I suppose my clothes fit about the same, maybe a little snug, but it's hard to tell. I mostly wear those elastic-waist slacks nowadays anyway. I buy them from that catalog that comes in the mail... oh, what is it called? Blair! Yes, Blair. They have the loveliest pastel colors for spring. I ordered a beautiful seafoam green pair last month, but when they arrived, the package was all torn up. I had to call the post office to complain, and the young man on the phone—Kevin, I think his name was—he was very polite, but he didn't seem to know what he was doing. It's like nobody takes pride in their work anymore. The mail carrier we used to have, Mr. Henderson, he was wonderful. He'd always make sure the packages were tucked safely behind the screen door so they wouldn't get rained on. He retired two years ago and moved to Florida to be near his grandchildren. I sent him a Christmas card last year, but I'm not sure if he got it. But, no, I haven't really noticed any big changes with my weight.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since this eating behavior started?\n\nPatient: Lost weight? Oh, heavens no. Definitely haven't lost any. Like I said, if anything, my clothes are a little tighter. But no, I haven't lost a single pound. \n\nNow, my sister-in-law Martha, she lost a whole lot of weight last year. She was doing one of those liquid diets, drinking those shakes... oh, what are they called? SlimFast! Yes. She drank the strawberry ones every single day. I told her, Martha, you need real food, you can't just live on strawberry milk. But she's always been so stubborn. Even when we were younger, back in the fifties, she would insist on wearing these dreadful pointy shoes that pinched her toes just because they were in fashion. She completely ruined her feet, you know. Has to wear special orthotics now in all her shoes. They cost her an absolute fortune because Medicare wouldn't cover them. \n\nIt's a crying shame what they won't cover these days, especially for eyes and teeth. I need a new crown on my back molar and Dr. Evans—he's my dentist, wonderful man, always asks about my hydrangeas—he quoted me a price that just about made my heart stop. I don't know how regular folks are supposed to afford it. \n\nBut... oh, what were you asking? Right, my weight. No, doctor, no weight loss for me.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that when you're eating those comfort foods, you're eating a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose it is a lot, now that you mention it. Some of those afternoons after choir practice, or when I come in from pulling those dreadful weeds in the garden, I'll go into the kitchen and fix myself a nice plate of rice and beans, or maybe some leftover chicken stew. But then... I just keep going back for more. I'll have another bowl, and then another, until I've probably eaten enough for two or three full meals in just a little bit of time. \n\nIt reminds me of how my late husband, Arthur, used to eat on Thanksgiving. Oh, Arthur could put away a turkey dinner like nobody's business! He always had to have three slices of my homemade pecan pie. I make it with real butter and dark corn syrup, none of that imitation stuff you get at the grocery store nowadays. The last time I was at the Piggly Wiggly, I couldn't even find real vanilla extract. It was all that artificial flavoring. And the prices! A tiny little bottle cost almost six dollars. I told the cashier—a sweet girl named Brittany, she's studying to be a nurse at the community college—I told her it was highway robbery. She just smiled and bagged my groceries. She always double-bags the heavy things for me, which I appreciate. My paper bags tore once in the driveway, and a jar of sweet pickles shattered everywhere. It took me an hour to hose down the concrete so the neighborhood stray cats wouldn't get sticky paws. \n\nBut yes, doctor, I do eat quite a large amount all at once when I get like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of your eating when you're having those large amounts of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: Well, that's exactly what brings me in, doctor. It's... it's like I have no control at all. Once I start eating those comfort foods, I'll sit there and tell myself, 'Maria, that is enough, you don't need another bite.' But my hand just goes right back to the pot anyway. It's like I can't make myself stop, no matter what I say in my head. \n\nIt's the strangest feeling, almost like when my car started acting up last winter. The brakes just wouldn't catch right. I was driving down Elm Street, right past the old Methodist church where they have those lovely stained glass windows. They restored them back in '98, I think it was. The Ladies Auxiliary raised the money through bake sales. Oh, the bake sales we used to have! My friend Betty would make these lemon bars that were just to die for. She wouldn't give anyone the recipe, took it right to the grave with her. Such a shame. Her daughter tried to recreate them for the church picnic last summer, but they were entirely too tart. I didn't say anything, of course, because you don't want to hurt anyone's feelings, especially at a church picnic. We had it out at Miller's Pond, and the mosquitoes were just terrible. I had bites all up and down my ankles for a week. I tried putting calamine lotion on them, but it just got all over my good floral bedsheets. \n\nBut yes, to answer your question, I don't feel in control at all. I just can't seem to stop myself once I get going.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or ashamed immediately after these episodes of eating large amounts of food?\n\nPatient: Oh, it's just awful. Yes, I do. Afterward, I just sit there and feel so ashamed and... well, just sad, really. Cooking and eating, it always used to mean family, you know? Taking care of Arthur and the boys, showing them I loved them. But now I've turned it into this terrible secret that I feel like I have to hide from everyone, especially the people at church. I'd just die if they knew. \n\nYou know how church ladies can talk. Margaret Higgins, bless her heart, she means well, but she has a mouth like a running faucet. Just last Sunday after service, she was going on and on about the choir director's new haircut. Said it made him look like a plucked chicken! I nearly choked on my peppermint. I always keep a few peppermints in my purse to keep my throat clear during the hymns. The red and white striped ones, you know the kind. Though I bought a bag of the green spearmint ones by mistake last month at the Dollar General. I ended up giving them to my neighbor's grandson, little Timmy. He's such a sweet boy, always helps me bring the empty trash cans back up the driveway on Tuesdays. The sanitation workers always leave them right in the middle of the sidewalk where a person could trip and break a hip. My friend Eleanor broke her hip back in 2015 just walking out to check her mail, and she was never the same. Had to move into one of those assisted living places over in Springfield, the one with the green awnings. \n\nBut, oh dear, what was I saying? Yes, the guilt. It's a very sad, heavy feeling to have to hide what I'm doing like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge or anxiety if you try to stop eating or resist the comfort foods when you're in the middle of one of these episodes?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I wouldn't say it's an overwhelming urge or anxiety, exactly. It's not like that fluttery feeling I get in my chest when I have to speak in front of the congregation. I had to read the announcements last month because Pastor Dave had a terrible head cold. Sounded like he was talking underwater! His wife, Susan, made him this awful-smelling onion syrup. She swears by it, says it clears the sinuses right up. She learned it from her grandmother who came over from Poland in the twenties. \n\nThey lived in Chicago for a long time before moving down here. Chicago! Can you imagine the wind? My sister-in-law went to Chicago once for a convention—she sold Mary Kay cosmetics for years. Had the pink Cadillac and everything. She used to give me all the samples of the lipstick colors that didn't sell well. There was this one shade, 'Frosted Watermelon,' that made my lips look like I'd been eating chalk. I wore it to a PTA meeting once back when my boys were in middle school, and the home economics teacher kept staring at my mouth the whole time. \n\nBut no, doctor, I don't really get anxious or have a strong urge like that if I try to stop. I just... I just don't stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like eating those comfort foods is something you have to do, even if you don't really want to?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I wouldn't say it's like a chore or something I'm being forced to do against my will. Usually, when I start, I really do want it. Like my macaroni and cheese, for instance. I always use the sharp cheddar from the deli counter at Kroger. Not the pre-packaged stuff in the plastic bags, mind you. That kind just never melts right. \n\nI learned that the hard way when I was making a casserole for the neighborhood block party a few years ago. The cashier at the store—this young girl with the brightest blue hair, just the exact color of a blue jay, can you imagine?—she accidentally gave me the mild cheddar instead of the sharp. I didn't want to hold up the line because the man behind me was buying three massive bags of dog food and looked like he was in a terrible rush, so I didn't check the label until I was already grating it at home. Now, my Arthur, God rest his soul, he wouldn't have known the difference. He'd eat just about anything as long as it had enough salt on it. His doctor was always getting after him about the salt shaker. He used to hide it behind the napkin holder whenever Dr. Evans would come up in conversation! Oh, he was a stubborn man, but I miss him terribly. \n\nBut no, to answer your question, I don't feel like I have to eat the food if I don't want it. I usually want it very much. It's just that once I take that first bite, the stopping part is what gets me.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating large amounts of food happen in a typical month?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think... Since my dear friend Eleanor passed away—oh, it's been so hard without her. We used to do everything together. Anyway, since she passed, this has been happening most weeks. Usually once or twice a week. It almost always happens on those quiet afternoons, you know? After I get home from choir practice on Wednesdays, or maybe after I've been out tending to the garden. \n\nMy hydrangeas are just beautiful this year, by the way. The big blue ones. I read in Better Homes and Gardens—or was it Southern Living? I get them both mixed up because the mail carrier, Mr. Henderson, always rubber-bands them together. He's retiring next month after thirty years on the route. His daughter is throwing him a big barbecue over at Centennial Park. She asked me to make my potato salad, the one with the little bits of chopped celery and paprika sprinkled on top. I always use Duke's mayonnaise, never Miracle Whip. Miracle Whip is just too sweet for a proper potato salad, don't you think? My aunt Betty used to put raisins in her potato salad, and I tell you, it was a crime against nature. We had to pretend to like it at every family reunion just to keep the peace. \n\nBut yes, to answer you, it's usually once or twice a week, on those quiet afternoons when the house just feels too empty.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself eating those comfort foods at the same time every day or week, like it's part of a routine?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. It's not like a strict schedule or anything. I don't set a clock for it like I do for my blood pressure pill. Dr. Evans is always so particular about me taking that at exactly 8:00 AM. He says consistency is key. Just like watering the tomatoes. If you don't water them at the same time every morning, before the sun gets too hot, they split right open. \n\nI had the most beautiful crop of Big Boy tomatoes last summer, just bursting on the vine. I canned twenty jars of marinara sauce. It's a recipe I got from my neighbor, Mrs. Rossi, years ago. She used to grow her own basil right on her kitchen windowsill in these little painted teacups. Her husband was a bricklayer, built that whole retaining wall down on Elm Street all by himself. He had hands like catchers' mitts, but he was so gentle when he'd prune her rosebushes. They moved to Florida back in '98, to one of those communities with the golf carts. I can't imagine driving a golf cart to the grocery store, can you? Where would you put all the bags? \n\nBut no, it's not a routine. It just sort of happens when the house is quiet and I'm feeling a bit lonely, not at a specific time.\n\nDoctor: Did you learn this eating behavior from someone in your family or is it something you developed on your own?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't really know if I learned it from anyone. I don't think so. My mother was always very proper about meals. We sat down at the table, all six of us, at exactly six o'clock every evening. She would ring this little brass bell when dinner was ready. It had a wooden handle that was worn smooth from years of use. I actually still have that bell, sitting right on my mantelpiece next to the porcelain dog Arthur won for me at the county fair in 1964. \n\nThat was the year they had the biggest Ferris wheel I'd ever seen. We rode it right at sunset, and you could see all the way out to the Miller farm. The Millers used to grow the sweetest corn. Silver Queen, it was called. You can't find corn like that anymore at the supermarket. It's all that engineered stuff now that tastes like cardboard. Anyway, my mother never would have eaten like I do now. She'd just have a small portion of whatever she cooked, and that was that. So, no, I suppose it's just something that started on my own, especially after things got so quiet around here.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating those comfort foods is a way for you to cope with your loneliness or other emotions?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't rightly know. I haven't really thought about it in those words, like coping with things. I suppose maybe? It's just... well, it's like when I sit down to watch my programs in the evening. I love watching those old black-and-white movies on that classic movie channel. Robert Osborne used to host them, you know. He had such a wonderful, soothing voice. I was just heartbroken when he passed away a few years back. He always knew the most fascinating little pieces of trivia about all the actors. Like Cary Grant! Did you know his real name was Archibald Leach? Just imagine trying to be a big romantic leading man with a name like Archibald. \n\nMy cousin Shirley's husband was named Archibald, but everyone just called him Archie. He ran the little hardware store over on Main Street for years and years, right up until that giant Home Depot moved in out on the highway and ran all the local places out of business. It's a real shame, it is. Archie used to keep a big glass jar of lollipops on the counter for the kids—the kind with the little safety loop handle so they wouldn't choke. They were always cherry or grape. I never cared much for the grape ones myself, they always tasted exactly like that thick purple cough syrup my mother used to make us take when we got the croup. \n\nBut oh, my goodness, I'm wandering again. What were you asking me? Something about my emotions? I'm just not sure, doctor. I just know the house gets awful quiet, and the next thing I know, I'm in the kitchen eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating behavior is something you've developed as a habit over time or is it related to how you were raised or taught to eat?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I kind of already mentioned my mother and how strict she was about our meals. So I don't think it's how I was raised. She would never have stood for anyone eating outside of supper time. She was very particular about manners, too. Elbows off the table, napkin in your lap. We had these lovely cloth napkins, not the paper ones you get nowadays. They were a crisp white linen with a little embroidered bluebird in the corner. My Aunt Clara made them for my mother as a wedding gift. \n\nAunt Clara was a seamstress down at the old textile mill before it closed in the seventies. She could sew anything, just by looking at a picture in a magazine. She made my prom dress, actually. It was a beautiful seafoam green chiffon with a sweetheart neckline. I went to the prom with Tommy Henderson. He drove his father's Buick, and we went to the diner afterward for milkshakes. Tommy ended up joining the Navy right after high school and I think he moved out to California. San Diego, maybe? Or was it San Francisco? I remember he sent me a postcard once with a picture of the Golden Gate Bridge. It always looked more orange than gold to me in the pictures. \n\nBut, oh, I'm sorry, I've lost the thread again. You were asking if it's a habit? I really couldn't say for sure. I just know it's something I do now when the house feels so empty.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you eat those comfort foods as a way to remember happier times or to fill the emptiness you feel?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm not entirely sure about that. Happier times? I suppose food does remind me of the old days sometimes. Like my grandmother's peach cobbler. Oh, she used to make it every Sunday after church during the summer. We went to the First Methodist down on Elm Street. The church had these gorgeous stained glass windows that the morning light would just pour through, making colorful little puddles on the wooden pews. They depicted the feeding of the five thousand, I think. Or maybe it was the wedding at Cana? \n\nReverend Higgins was the pastor back then. He had this booming voice that would wake you right up if you started dozing during the sermon, but he was really the gentlest man. He always kept peppermint candies in his coat pocket for the children. Not the soft ones, mind you, but the hard ones with the little red and white stripes wrapped in clear cellophane. You had to be careful not to bite down on them too soon or you'd crack a tooth! My brother Jimmy did exactly that once, chipped his front tooth right in half the week before school picture day. Mother was absolutely furious with him. She tried to make him smile with his lips closed for the photographer, but he just looked like he was holding his breath or trying not to sneeze. Oh, Jimmy... he was always getting into some sort of scrape. \n\nBut goodness me, I'm rambling on again about Jimmy's teeth. What were we talking about? Filling the emptiness? I really couldn't tell you if that's why I do it, doctor. I haven't figured all that out. I just know I end up eating when there's nothing else going on and the house is just too quiet.\n\nDoctor: Do you think eating those comfort foods is something you've learned to do over time as a way to deal with your emotions?\n\nPatient: Learned to do it over time? I really couldn't say. I never was much of a student when it came to learning things, to be perfectly honest. I actually left high school in the tenth grade to help out at home, and then I got a job at the Woolworth's lunch counter down in the square. Oh, they had us wear these stiff pink cotton uniforms with a white apron that you had to iron and starch every single night. If the manager, Mr. Abernathy, saw even a hint of a wrinkle, he'd send you right to the back room. \n\nMr. Abernathy was a very particular man. He wore a silver pocket watch on a chain that he'd pull out and check constantly. He said it belonged to his grandfather, who fought in the Spanish-American War. Or maybe it was World War I? I remember he told us the whole story once when the soda fountain broke down and we had to wait all afternoon for the repairman. The repairman's name was Earl, and he always smelled like peppermint oil and motor grease. He drove this big yellow truck that rattled so loud you could hear him coming from three streets over. \n\nAnyway, I'm sorry, my mind is just a million miles away today. Did you ask if I learned to do it? I just don't know about all that, doctor. I just know that when I'm sitting there by myself in the evening and the television isn't enough to keep me company, I end up getting into the food.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that eating those comfort foods is something you've learned to do as a way to deal with your feelings over time?\n\nPatient: Well, you're asking me that again, and... I honestly don't know if it's something I learned. Learned to deal with feelings? I don't think people talked about dealing with feelings back when I was coming up. You just got on with things. Like when my husband, Harold, passed. He worked at the post office for thirty-five years. Started out sorting mail in the back room and worked his way up to the front counter. He knew everyone's name in town, and their dogs' names, too. \n\nHe used to carry those little milk-bone biscuits in his pockets for the dogs on his route back when he was walking. The pockets of his uniform trousers would get all crumbly and dusty from them. I spent half my life turning those pockets inside out before putting them in the wash. We had this old wringer washer on the back porch... oh, it was a beast of a machine. You had to be so careful feeding the clothes through the rollers or it would grab your fingers right along with the shirts. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable, caught her whole hand in hers once. She had to wear a cast for six weeks and her daughter had to come over every day to help her pin the laundry to the line. \n\nBut... oh dear, I'm doing it again, aren't I? Going on and on. You were asking about learning to deal with feelings? I just... I don't have a good answer for you. I just know that when I'm feeling lonely, the food is there, and I eat it. I don't know if I learned it or if it just happened.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
